========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I’m here because things at home have gotten really hard since my in-laws moved into our house. I mean, I knew it would be an adjustment, and I kept telling myself I could handle it, but I feel like I’m constantly keyed up, like I can’t ever fully relax in my own space. And then at the same time I’m just worn down all the time.

It’s starting to spill over in ways that don’t feel like me. I’ve been snapping at my wife over things I would normally let go, or at least talk through better. And with work—I manage artists, musicians, that kind of thing—I’m usually on top of calls and contracts immediately, but lately I’ve been avoiding calls I know I need to take. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it properly. That scared me, honestly. It made me think, okay, this isn’t just normal stress anymore.

Yes—honestly, it feels like a lot of the day now. Not always fear like something terrible is about to happen, but I’m keyed up almost constantly, like I’m bracing for the next interruption or the next thing going wrong at home. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. I mean… maybe it can feel that way in the moment, because once I’m tense, my mind starts jumping around a bit. But it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying equally about every possible area of life.

It’s more that I feel generally overloaded, and then whatever is in front of me can become the thing I’m stuck on. So if something happens at home, that takes over. If I’m thinking about work, then I start turning that over too. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.

It’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.

And at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.

It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.

At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.

So… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.

I mean, I do feel anxious and tense a lot, but it’s more like this ongoing pressure or being on edge, not like a sudden episode that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast.\n\nThere have been moments where I feel overwhelmed, sure, like I need to step away or I can’t take one more thing in that moment, but I don’t know that it reaches this intense peak within minutes the way you’re asking. At least, I haven’t really noticed a distinct “attack” or something like that. It’s more constant than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. I mean… maybe it can feel that way in the moment, because once I’m tense, my mind starts jumping around a bit. But it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying equally about every possible area of life.\n\nIt’s more that I feel generally overloaded, and then whatever is in front of me can become the thing I’m stuck on. So if something happens at home, that takes over. If I’m thinking about work, then I start turning that over too. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.\n\nIt’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. So yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.

But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.\n\nIt’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. Since they moved in, I haven’t felt like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate, because I know she’s dealing with this too. And at work, I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle right away. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. I mean, I can worry like anyone does, and my work has plenty of moving pieces, but this doesn’t feel like I’m just randomly anxious when nothing is going on.

It feels very tied to what happened at home. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve just been… keyed up and worn down at the same time. Like my nervous system never gets to clock out. And that’s when I started snapping at my wife, which really bothers me, because that’s not who I want to be with her. And then it bled into work too—I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. That is not like me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.

So I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying excessively about everyday things when there’s no obvious stressor. It’s more that this one situation has overloaded me so much that everything around it has started to feel harder to manage.

I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think clearly enough to handle it. So yes, anxious is probably the right word. It’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel anxious and tense a lot, but it’s more like this ongoing pressure or being on edge, not like a sudden episode that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast.\n\nThere have been moments where I feel overwhelmed, sure, like I need to step away or I can’t take one more thing in that moment, but I don’t know that it reaches this intense peak within minutes the way you’re asking. At least, I haven’t really noticed a distinct “attack” or something like that. It’s more constant than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. I mean… maybe it can feel that way in the moment, because once I’m tense, my mind starts jumping around a bit. But it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying equally about every possible area of life.\n\nIt’s more that I feel generally overloaded, and then whatever is in front of me can become the thing I’m stuck on. So if something happens at home, that takes over. If I’m thinking about work, then I start turning that over too. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.\n\nIt’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. Since they moved in, I haven’t felt like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate, because I know she’s dealing with this too. And at work, I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle right away. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about everyday things even when there aren't any obvious stressors present?\n\nPatient: Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. I mean, I can worry like anyone does, and my work has plenty of moving pieces, but this doesn’t feel like I’m just randomly anxious when nothing is going on.\n\nIt feels very tied to what happened at home. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve just been… keyed up and worn down at the same time. Like my nervous system never gets to clock out. And that’s when I started snapping at my wife, which really bothers me, because that’s not who I want to be with her. And then it bled into work too—I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. That is not like me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying excessively about everyday things when there’s no obvious stressor. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think if the in-law situation got resolved, I’d still have normal stress, sure. My job isn’t exactly calm, and life doesn’t just become simple because one thing changes. But I really don’t think I’d feel anxious in this same constant, grinding way.

The reason I say that is because this whole thing really started after they moved into our house. Since then I’ve felt so keyed up and worn down, like I never fully get a break. And it’s not just me feeling uncomfortable in my own head—it’s changed how I’m acting. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate. I’ve been avoiding work calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not how I operate. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.

So if that one stressor was resolved, even if everything else stayed the same, I think a big part of this anxiety would lift. Maybe not all stress, but this level of not functioning like myself—I don’t think that would still be there the same way.

No, I don’t think so—not to this extent. I mean, I’d still worry about things, because that’s part of my life and my job. There are always contracts, calls, schedules, people needing decisions quickly… so I’m not saying I’d suddenly become completely calm about everything.

Since they moved in, I just haven’t felt like I have any space to reset. And it’s started showing up in ways that honestly scare me a little, because they’re not typical for me. I’m snapping at my wife, and I hate that. I’ve been avoiding calls I’d normally handle right away. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.

So if that situation were resolved and nothing else changed, I think I’d still have normal stress, but I don’t think I’d be worrying or falling apart like this. This feels like it’s coming from that specific pressure at home.

But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.\n\nIt’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. Since they moved in, I haven’t felt like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate, because I know she’s dealing with this too. And at work, I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle right away. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about everyday things even when there aren't any obvious stressors present?\n\nPatient: Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. I mean, I can worry like anyone does, and my work has plenty of moving pieces, but this doesn’t feel like I’m just randomly anxious when nothing is going on.\n\nIt feels very tied to what happened at home. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve just been… keyed up and worn down at the same time. Like my nervous system never gets to clock out. And that’s when I started snapping at my wife, which really bothers me, because that’s not who I want to be with her. And then it bled into work too—I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. That is not like me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying excessively about everyday things when there’s no obvious stressor. It’s more that this one situation has overloaded me so much that everything around it has started to feel harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if the situation with your in-laws was the only stressor in your life that got resolved, but other parts of your life remained unchanged?\n\nPatient: I think if the in-law situation got resolved, I’d still have normal stress, sure. My job isn’t exactly calm, and life doesn’t just become simple because one thing changes. But I really don’t think I’d feel anxious in this same constant, grinding way.\n\nThe reason I say that is because this whole thing really started after they moved into our house. Since then I’ve felt so keyed up and worn down, like I never fully get a break. And it’s not just me feeling uncomfortable in my own head—it’s changed how I’m acting. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate. I’ve been avoiding work calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not how I operate. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that one stressor was resolved, even if everything else stayed the same, I think a big part of this anxiety would lift. Maybe not all stress, but this level of not functioning like myself—I don’t think that would still be there the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about other things to this extent if the situation with your in-laws was resolved and nothing else in your life changed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not to this extent. I mean, I’d still worry about things, because that’s part of my life and my job. There are always contracts, calls, schedules, people needing decisions quickly… so I’m not saying I’d suddenly become completely calm about everything.\n\nBut this level of worry, this feeling like I’m constantly keyed up and completely worn down at the same time, really feels connected to my in-laws being in the house. Since they moved in, I just haven’t felt like I have any space to reset. And it’s started showing up in ways that honestly scare me a little, because they’re not typical for me. I’m snapping at my wife, and I hate that. I’ve been avoiding calls I’d normally handle right away. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that situation were resolved and nothing else changed, I think I’d still have normal stress, but I don’t think I’d be worrying or falling apart like this. This feels like it’s coming from that specific pressure at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

This feels like it’s coming from that specific pressure at home.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you could find ways to cope with or adapt to your in-laws living with you, without them actually moving out?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / agent_or_business_manager_of_artist_performer_or_athlete / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that I’m snapping at my wife, avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately, and I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it would make everything disappear, because they’d still be in the house and that’s the source of so much of this. But if I could actually find a way to cope with it, or adapt to it without feeling like I’m constantly bracing myself, then yes, I think my anxiety would be significantly reduced.

Because the problem isn’t just that they’re physically there, it’s what it’s doing to me day after day. Since my in-laws moved in, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that I don’t feel like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate even saying out loud, because she’s not the enemy here. And at work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally just handle immediately. That is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.

So if coping better meant I could get some sense of space back, or not feel so constantly overloaded, then yes, I think that would help a lot. Maybe they wouldn’t have to move out for me to feel better, but something would have to change in how I’m managing it, because the way it is now is just not sustainable.

I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it properly. That scared me, honestly. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. My job isn’t exactly calm, and life doesn’t just become simple because one thing changes. I don’t think it would persist in the same way, no. I mean, I’m sure I’d still have stress — I don’t have a stress-free life, and my work has plenty of pressure built into it — but this particular level of anxiety feels tied to what’s happening at home.

Maybe some tension would still be there because they’d still be in the house, but I don’t think I’d be falling apart or operating this badly if I had a real way to manage it.

I mean, I do feel anxious and tense a lot, but it’s more like this ongoing pressure or being on edge, not like a sudden episode that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast.\n\nThere have been moments where I feel overwhelmed, sure, like I need to step away or I can’t take one more thing in that moment, but I don’t know that it reaches this intense peak within minutes the way you’re asking. At least, I haven’t really noticed a distinct “attack” or something like that. It’s more constant than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about everyday things even when there aren't any obvious stressors present?\n\nPatient: Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. And with work—I manage artists, musicians, that kind of thing—I’m usually on top of calls and contracts immediately, but lately I’ve been avoiding calls I know I need to take. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it properly. That scared me, honestly. It made me think, okay, this isn’t just normal stress anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or fearful in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes—honestly, it feels like a lot of the day now. Not always fear like something terrible is about to happen, but I’m keyed up almost constantly, like I’m bracing for the next interruption or the next thing going wrong at home. Since my in-laws moved in, I don’t really feel settled in my own house, and that tension just follows me around.\n\nAnd then I get worn down from it, which makes everything worse. I’ll snap at my wife over things I know I wouldn’t normally react to that way, and then I feel awful about it. At work, I’m usually very responsive—I manage artists and musicians, so calls and contracts can’t just sit there—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I feel too overwhelmed to deal with them. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think clearly enough to handle it. So yes, anxious is probably the right word. It’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel anxious and tense a lot, but it’s more like this ongoing pressure or being on edge, not like a sudden episode that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast.\n\nThere have been moments where I feel overwhelmed, sure, like I need to step away or I can’t take one more thing in that moment, but I don’t know that it reaches this intense peak within minutes the way you’re asking. At least, I haven’t really noticed a distinct “attack” or something like that. It’s more constant than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. I mean… maybe it can feel that way in the moment, because once I’m tense, my mind starts jumping around a bit. But it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying equally about every possible area of life.\n\nIt’s more that I feel generally overloaded, and then whatever is in front of me can become the thing I’m stuck on. So if something happens at home, that takes over. If I’m thinking about work, then I start turning that over too. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.\n\nIt’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. Since they moved in, I haven’t felt like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate, because I know she’s dealing with this too. And at work, I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle right away. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about everyday things even when there aren't any obvious stressors present?\n\nPatient: Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. I mean, I can worry like anyone does, and my work has plenty of moving pieces, but this doesn’t feel like I’m just randomly anxious when nothing is going on.\n\nIt feels very tied to what happened at home. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve just been… keyed up and worn down at the same time. Like my nervous system never gets to clock out. And that’s when I started snapping at my wife, which really bothers me, because that’s not who I want to be with her. And then it bled into work too—I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. That is not like me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying excessively about everyday things when there’s no obvious stressor. It’s more that this one situation has overloaded me so much that everything around it has started to feel harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if the situation with your in-laws was the only stressor in your life that got resolved, but other parts of your life remained unchanged?\n\nPatient: I think if the in-law situation got resolved, I’d still have normal stress, sure. My job isn’t exactly calm, and life doesn’t just become simple because one thing changes. But I really don’t think I’d feel anxious in this same constant, grinding way.\n\nThe reason I say that is because this whole thing really started after they moved into our house. Since then I’ve felt so keyed up and worn down, like I never fully get a break. And it’s not just me feeling uncomfortable in my own head—it’s changed how I’m acting. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate. I’ve been avoiding work calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not how I operate. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that one stressor was resolved, even if everything else stayed the same, I think a big part of this anxiety would lift. Maybe not all stress, but this level of not functioning like myself—I don’t think that would still be there the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about other things to this extent if the situation with your in-laws was resolved and nothing else in your life changed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not to this extent. I mean, I’d still worry about things, because that’s part of my life and my job. There are always contracts, calls, schedules, people needing decisions quickly… so I’m not saying I’d suddenly become completely calm about everything.\n\nBut this level of worry, this feeling like I’m constantly keyed up and completely worn down at the same time, really feels connected to my in-laws being in the house. Since they moved in, I just haven’t felt like I have any space to reset. And it’s started showing up in ways that honestly scare me a little, because they’re not typical for me. I’m snapping at my wife, and I hate that. I’ve been avoiding calls I’d normally handle right away. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that situation were resolved and nothing else changed, I think I’d still have normal stress, but I don’t think I’d be worrying or falling apart like this. This feels like it’s coming from that specific pressure at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you could find ways to cope with or adapt to your in-laws living with you, without them actually moving out?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. But if I could actually find a way to cope with it, or adapt to it without feeling like I’m constantly bracing myself, then yes, I think my anxiety would be significantly reduced.\n\nBecause the problem isn’t just that they’re physically there, it’s what it’s doing to me day after day. And at work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally just handle immediately. That is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if coping better meant I could get some sense of space back, or not feel so constantly overloaded, then yes, I think that would help a lot. Maybe they wouldn’t have to move out for me to feel better, but something would have to change in how I’m managing it, because the way it is now is just not sustainable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would persist even if you found effective ways to manage the stress related to your in-laws living with you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would persist in the same way, no. I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not like me at all. It’s mostly about my in-laws living with us. I don’t want to make it sound like the rest of my life is perfectly calm, because it isn’t — my work can be intense, and there are always things that need attention right away — but this level of anxiety, this feeling like I’m constantly wound up and exhausted, started with them being in the house.

I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think clearly enough to handle it. So yes, anxious is probably the right word. It’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do feel anxious and tense a lot, but it’s more like this ongoing pressure or being on edge, not like a sudden episode that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast.\n\nThere have been moments where I feel overwhelmed, sure, like I need to step away or I can’t take one more thing in that moment, but I don’t know that it reaches this intense peak within minutes the way you’re asking. At least, I haven’t really noticed a distinct “attack” or something like that. It’s more constant than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. I mean… maybe it can feel that way in the moment, because once I’m tense, my mind starts jumping around a bit. But it’s not like I’m sitting there worrying equally about every possible area of life.\n\nIt’s more that I feel generally overloaded, and then whatever is in front of me can become the thing I’m stuck on. So if something happens at home, that takes over. If I’m thinking about work, then I start turning that over too. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as constant worry about a wide range of unrelated things, if that’s what you mean. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. Since my in-laws moved in, there’s this constant sense that I’m bracing for something—another interruption, another conversation, another problem I’m supposed to handle calmly when I already feel completely worn down.\n\nIt’s not just mental, either. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. Since they moved in, I haven’t felt like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate, because I know she’s dealing with this too. And at work, I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle right away. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about everyday things even when there aren't any obvious stressors present?\n\nPatient: Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. I mean, I can worry like anyone does, and my work has plenty of moving pieces, but this doesn’t feel like I’m just randomly anxious when nothing is going on.\n\nIt feels very tied to what happened at home. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve just been… keyed up and worn down at the same time. Like my nervous system never gets to clock out. And that’s when I started snapping at my wife, which really bothers me, because that’s not who I want to be with her. And then it bled into work too—I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. That is not like me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying excessively about everyday things when there’s no obvious stressor. It’s more that this one situation has overloaded me so much that everything around it has started to feel harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if the situation with your in-laws was the only stressor in your life that got resolved, but other parts of your life remained unchanged?\n\nPatient: I think if the in-law situation got resolved, I’d still have normal stress, sure. My job isn’t exactly calm, and life doesn’t just become simple because one thing changes. But I really don’t think I’d feel anxious in this same constant, grinding way.\n\nThe reason I say that is because this whole thing really started after they moved into our house. Since then I’ve felt so keyed up and worn down, like I never fully get a break. And it’s not just me feeling uncomfortable in my own head—it’s changed how I’m acting. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate. I’ve been avoiding work calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not how I operate. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that one stressor was resolved, even if everything else stayed the same, I think a big part of this anxiety would lift. Maybe not all stress, but this level of not functioning like myself—I don’t think that would still be there the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about other things to this extent if the situation with your in-laws was resolved and nothing else in your life changed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not to this extent. I mean, I’d still worry about things, because that’s part of my life and my job. There are always contracts, calls, schedules, people needing decisions quickly… so I’m not saying I’d suddenly become completely calm about everything.\n\nBut this level of worry, this feeling like I’m constantly keyed up and completely worn down at the same time, really feels connected to my in-laws being in the house. Since they moved in, I just haven’t felt like I have any space to reset. And it’s started showing up in ways that honestly scare me a little, because they’re not typical for me. I’m snapping at my wife, and I hate that. I’ve been avoiding calls I’d normally handle right away. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that situation were resolved and nothing else changed, I think I’d still have normal stress, but I don’t think I’d be worrying or falling apart like this. This feels like it’s coming from that specific pressure at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you could find ways to cope with or adapt to your in-laws living with you, without them actually moving out?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it would make everything disappear, because they’d still be in the house and that’s the source of so much of this. But if I could actually find a way to cope with it, or adapt to it without feeling like I’m constantly bracing myself, then yes, I think my anxiety would be significantly reduced.\n\nBecause the problem isn’t just that they’re physically there, it’s what it’s doing to me day after day. Since my in-laws moved in, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that I don’t feel like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate even saying out loud, because she’s not the enemy here. And at work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally just handle immediately. That is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if coping better meant I could get some sense of space back, or not feel so constantly overloaded, then yes, I think that would help a lot. Maybe they wouldn’t have to move out for me to feel better, but something would have to change in how I’m managing it, because the way it is now is just not sustainable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would persist even if you found effective ways to manage the stress related to your in-laws living with you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would persist in the same way, no. I mean, I’m sure I’d still have stress — I don’t have a stress-free life, and my work has plenty of pressure built into it — but this particular level of anxiety feels tied to what’s happening at home.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that it’s started changing how I function. I’m snapping at my wife, which makes me feel awful. I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not like me at all. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if I actually found effective ways to manage the stress of them living with us — not just telling myself to deal with it, but really being able to cope — then I think the anxiety would drop a lot. Maybe some tension would still be there because they’d still be in the house, but I don’t think I’d be falling apart or operating this badly if I had a real way to manage it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety is mostly about your in-laws living with you or is it more about everything in your life in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about my in-laws living with us. I don’t want to make it sound like the rest of my life is perfectly calm, because it isn’t — my work can be intense, and there are always things that need attention right away — but this level of anxiety, this feeling like I’m constantly wound up and exhausted, started with them being in the house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that I’m snapping at my wife, avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately, and I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight. That’s not how I usually operate. I’m used to stress, but this feels different. It feels like I don’t get any space to recover, and then it spills into everything else. So yes, I’d say it’s mainly about that situation at home, not just general life stress.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And at work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally just handle immediately. That is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if coping better meant I could get some sense of space back, or not feel so constantly overloaded, then yes, I think that would help a lot. Maybe they wouldn’t have to move out for me to feel better, but something would have to change in how I’m managing it, because the way it is now is just not sustainable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would persist even if you found effective ways to manage the stress related to your in-laws living with you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would persist in the same way, no. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that it’s started changing how I function. I’m snapping at my wife, which makes me feel awful. I’m avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not like me at all. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if I actually found effective ways to manage the stress of them living with us — not just telling myself to deal with it, but really being able to cope — then I think the anxiety would drop a lot. Maybe some tension would still be there because they’d still be in the house, but I don’t think I’d be falling apart or operating this badly if I had a real way to manage it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety is mostly about your in-laws living with you or is it more about everything in your life in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about my in-laws living with us. I don’t want to make it sound like the rest of my life is perfectly calm, because it isn’t — my work can be intense, and there are always things that need attention right away — but this level of anxiety, this feeling like I’m constantly wound up and exhausted, started with them being in the house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that I’m snapping at my wife, avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately, and I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight. That’s not how I usually operate. I’m used to stress, but this feels different. It feels like I don’t get any space to recover, and then it spills into everything else. So yes, I’d say it’s mainly about that situation at home, not just general life stress.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still worry excessively about everyday things if the situation with your in-laws living with you was completely resolved?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so — not excessively, anyway. I mean, I worry about everyday things like anyone does, especially with my kind of work where there are always calls and contracts and people needing answers right away. But this feels different. This feels like it really started because of the situation at home.

Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve been so keyed up and worn down that I’m not functioning like myself. I’m snapping at my wife, which really bothers me, and I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally just deal with immediately. I even nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight. That’s the part that scares me, because I’m usually on top of those things.

So if the living situation with my in-laws was completely resolved, I think a lot of this constant worry and tension would ease up. I’m sure I’d still have normal stress, but I don’t think I’d be worrying excessively about everyday things in the same way.

At least, I haven’t really noticed a distinct “attack” or something like that. It’s more constant than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things at the same time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “a lot of different things” exactly. It feels more tied to what’s been going on and how hard it’s been to manage everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, restless is a good word for it. I feel like I can’t really settle, even in my own house. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should get up, check something, avoid something, answer something, but then I’m too overwhelmed to actually deal with it properly. At work, that’s really not like me. I normally handle calls right away—especially with artists and musicians, because things can fall apart fast if you don’t stay on top of them—but lately I’ve been avoiding calls because I just feel too keyed up to think straight. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t focus and respond the way I usually would.\n\nAnd at home, the restlessness comes out as irritability. I snap at my wife, and then I hate myself for it, because I know she’s not the enemy. I’m just… on edge all the time, like I don’t have a place to put all this tension.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about the situation at home with your in-laws or is it more about everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the situation at home, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I had zero stress before—my job can be intense anyway, dealing with artists and musicians and contracts and people needing answers quickly—but I was managing it. I was used to that kind of pressure.\n\nSince my in-laws moved into our house, though, it’s like I just can’t get back to baseline. I’m keyed up all the time and worn down at the same time, if that makes sense. And it’s spilling into everything else. I’m snapping at my wife over things I normally wouldn’t, and then I feel terrible afterward. At work, I’ve been avoiding calls I would usually handle immediately, which is very unlike me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo… yes, the anxiety feels rooted in what’s going on at home, but it’s not staying neatly contained there. It’s started affecting everything around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if the situation with your in-laws living with you were to resolve?\n\nPatient: Yes… I really think it would. I mean, I can’t say every bit of stress in my life would magically disappear, because work is still work and there are always things to juggle. But the way I’ve been feeling lately—the keyed-up, worn-down feeling—it really started after my in-laws moved into our house.\n\nThat’s why I’m here, honestly. That is very unlike me. I’m usually on top of things, especially with musicians and contracts, because if you let things sit too long they can unravel. And I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I just couldn’t think clearly enough to deal with it.\n\nSo yes, if that situation at home resolved, I think the anxiety would decrease a lot. I think I’d at least have a chance to calm down and get back to functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about everyday things even when there aren't any obvious stressors present?\n\nPatient: Not really in that broad, everything-all-the-time way, no. I mean, I can worry like anyone does, and my work has plenty of moving pieces, but this doesn’t feel like I’m just randomly anxious when nothing is going on.\n\nIt feels very tied to what happened at home. Since my in-laws moved into our house, I’ve just been… keyed up and worn down at the same time. Like my nervous system never gets to clock out. And then it bled into work too—I’ve been avoiding calls I would normally handle immediately. That is not like me. I nearly let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying excessively about everyday things when there’s no obvious stressor. It’s more that this one situation has overloaded me so much that everything around it has started to feel harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if the situation with your in-laws was the only stressor in your life that got resolved, but other parts of your life remained unchanged?\n\nPatient: I think if the in-law situation got resolved, I’d still have normal stress, sure. My job isn’t exactly calm, and life doesn’t just become simple because one thing changes. But I really don’t think I’d feel anxious in this same constant, grinding way.\n\nThe reason I say that is because this whole thing really started after they moved into our house. Since then I’ve felt so keyed up and worn down, like I never fully get a break. And it’s not just me feeling uncomfortable in my own head—it’s changed how I’m acting. I’m snapping at my wife, which I hate. I’ve been avoiding work calls I would normally handle immediately, and that’s not how I operate. I almost let a musician’s contract fall apart because I couldn’t think straight enough to deal with it.\n\nSo if that one stressor was resolved, even if everything else stayed the same, I think a big part of this anxiety would lift. Maybe not all stress, but this level of not functioning like myself—I don’t think that would still be there the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about other things to this extent if the situation with your in-laws was resolved and nothing else in your life changed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not to this extent.
